C&C

A Corporate Fantasy Campaign for Characters of Levels 1–20

# The Franchise Wars

*Questionable Employment, Hostile Takeovers & Heroic Customer Service*

> *“Adventure is dangerous. Employment is mandatory.”— QuestRabbit Adventuring Solutions Employee Handbook, Section 1*

**24**Spine Missions

**13**Corporations

**68**Franchises

**168**Contracts

**13**Receipts

Pacts & Polyhedrals

Chapter 1

## Introduction

The age of the independent tavern is dying. Across the kingdoms, corporations have discovered something far more powerful than dragons, archmages or divine favour: **brand recognition**. Coffee is bought from wizarding conglomerates. Inns run on enchanted loyalty schemes. Necromancers handle the night shift. And when something goes wrong, they hire adventurers.

*The Franchise Wars* is a comedic corporate-fantasy campaign for the fifth edition of the world's greatest roleplaying game, taking a party of contractors from 1st to 20th level. The characters sign with **QuestRabbit Adventuring Solutions**, a cheerful employment agency that takes thirty per cent, and are sent into the kitchens, cellars, stockrooms and boardrooms of sixty-eight franchises owned by a dozen holding companies that all, eventually, answer to one.

It begins with a burrito. It ends with a contract for the Material Plane, and a blank line at the bottom waiting for the characters' names.

### What's in This Book

**Chapter 1** (this chapter) explains the premise, the tone and the shape of the campaign. **Chapter 2** presents the campaign's systems: the Rabbit Rating, Leverage, the Commons Tally, Receipts and the Final Contract. **Chapter 3** is a gazetteer of Brandmere, the franchise city where the campaign is based. **Chapter 4** contains the twenty-four **Spine Missions**, the interlocking adventures that carry the plot from level 1 to level 20. **Chapter 5** profiles the thirteen **corporate entities**, from Creation & Company down to the Last Independents, each in the same dossier format as the franchises they own. **Chapter 6** is the **Franchise Directory**: sixty-eight businesses, each with a flagship, menu, secret and contracts. The appendices hold the bestiary, magic items, the full text of the Final Contract, the campaign flowchart and the DM's tables.

### The Shape of the Campaign

The campaign is built on a **spine** of twenty-four missions divided into five acts. Around the spine hang well over a hundred **side contracts**, drawn from every franchise and corporation in the book. The spine tells the story; the side contracts let the party choose which companies to help, betray, rob or save, and they generate the **Leverage** that decides who stands with the party at the end.

Every spine mission that matters leaves the party holding a **Receipt**: a piece of evidence, a document, a witness, a thing. In the final session, Tallis Vend of Creation & Company lays the Final Contract on the table. It has thirteen clauses. Each Receipt voids one. A party that paid attention since level 1 walks into that room with a folder full of paper and tears the thing apart. A party that didn't has to argue.

### The Central Secret

DM Only — The Truth of C&C





### Tone: The Golden Rule of Corporate Quests

Every corporate adventure begins with language that makes something horrifying sound completely ordinary. The monsters are real and the dungeons are deadly, but the thing the players should dread most is a memo. Each spine mission opens with one. Read it aloud, deadpan.

| Never say…Instead, Corporate says…         |                                                                                                                            |
| ------------------------------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| “A demon has possessed our restaurant.”    | **THAC0 SPELL INCIDENT REPORT 77-B.** Unscheduled lower-kitchen personnel reassignment.                                    |
| “Our gelatinous cube ate three employees.” | **CUSTOMER RETENTION EVENT.** Three team members currently remain within company property outside scheduled working hours. |
| “The CEO became a lich.”                   | **EXECUTIVE CONTINUITY INITIATIVE SUCCESSFULLY IMPLEMENTED.**                                                              |
| “Kill our competitors.”                    | Qualified contractors are requested to facilitate an accelerated reduction in competitor market presence.                  |
| “You'll probably die.”                     | This role offers significant exposure to growth opportunities.                                                             |
| “We're not paying you.”                    | Remuneration will be processed in the next applicable cycle.                                                               |

Play the corporate world straight and the jokes land by themselves. Let the humour dominate early; let it curdle, slowly, as the party realises the joke has been about them all along. By Act V, a cheerful onboarding pamphlet should be the scariest prop at the table.

### Running Side Contracts

Between spine missions, the job board at the Warren shows whatever contracts suit the party's level. Every contract in this book follows the same format: a **Brief** (what Corporate says), the **Truth** (what's actually happening), how to **Run** it, the **Opposition**, the **Pay** (always quoted before QuestRabbit's 30%), a **Thread** that ties it into the wider story, and a **Leverage** target. A good rhythm is one or two side contracts per spine mission in Acts I–II, one per mission in Acts III–IV, and side contracts only as “last holdout” scenes in Act V.

### Safety & Satire

The franchises parody real brands, and the villain is capitalism wearing a wizard's hat. Aim the satire upward, at Corporate, never at the underpaid people behind the counter. Every franchise in this book has at least one employee who is doing their best. Let the players find them. They are the Commons Tally, and they are how the campaign is won.

Chapter 2

## Campaign Systems

The campaign uses five tracking systems. None is complicated; together they make every side contract matter to the finale. Keep a single sheet—the **Contractor File**—on which you record all five.

### Contract Grades

| GradeLevelsTypical WorkBase Pay |       |                                                                                   |                  |
| ------------------------------- | ----- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------- |
| **Copper**                      | 1–4   | Pest control, deliveries, missing employees, petty espionage                      | 25–150 gp        |
| **Silver**                      | 5–8   | Monster nests, sabotage, dangerous acquisitions, supernatural workplace incidents | 300–1,500 gp     |
| **Gold**                        | 9–12  | Interplanar procurement, corporate warfare, powerful magical entities             | 2,000–8,000 gp   |
| **Platinum**                    | 13–16 | Demonic mergers, dimensional monopolies, ancient franchises, catastrophes         | 10,000–40,000 gp |
| **Black Card**                  | 17–20 | Jobs that technically never existed                                               | Payment in kind  |

**The 30%.** QuestRabbit deducts thirty per cent of every fee. Pip Ashworth will explain that this is industry standard. It is not. Players who negotiate directly with a client (Persuasion DC 15 + the grade's tier) can cut QuestRabbit out—but a contract taken off the board doesn't count toward the Rabbit Rating.

### The Rabbit Rating

The party shares a single rating from one to five stars. It begins at one star when they sign the Contractor Agreement in S1. Publicly, it's a customer-satisfaction score that unlocks better contracts. Secretly, it measures how much ordinary people trust the party—and it is the metaphysical threshold that makes their signatures valid for the Material Plane.

| StarsUnlocksHidden Meaning (DM) |                                                                  |                                                                        |
| ------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| ★                               | Copper contracts; a badge                                        | Known to one neighbourhood                                             |
| ★★                              | Silver contracts; QuestRabbit expense account (50 gp/month)      | Trusted by a district                                                  |
| ★★★                             | Gold contracts; a Folio page is opened in the party's name (S10) | Trusted by a city                                                      |
| ★★★★                            | Platinum contracts; a private office at the Warren               | Trusted by a nation                                                    |
| ★★★★★                           | Black Card; an invitation to the AGM (S20)                       | Representatives of the Commons: signature valid for the Material Plane |

**Earning Stars.** The rating is not XP. It rises at the milestones shown in the spine (S2, S5, S10, S15 and S20 each add a star if the party completed the act's missions) and it can rise early if the party does something that makes *the public* love them. It never falls below the star count the spine requires—the Ledger won't allow it. Players who try to tank their rating deliberately (rudeness, sabotage, refusing jobs) discover that one-star reviews simply don't post. This is the first clue, around Act III, that the rating isn't what it seems.

**The Mandate (optional).** If the party cuts ties with QuestRabbit, the rating continues under a new name: the *Mandate*, tracked in the Folio page Wand-Mart opens for them in S10. Nothing changes except the font.

### Leverage

Leverage represents dirt, debts, favours and secrets the party holds over a specific corporation. Track Leverage separately for each of the ten Board umbrellas plus Creation & Company. Every contract in this book lists a Leverage target; completing it with the truth exposed (not buried) grants **1 Leverage** against that target. Spine missions grant the amounts they list.

**Spending Leverage.** A point can be spent at any time to gain one of the following against the target corporation, its staff or its directors:

- **Advantage.** Advantage on one Charisma check, or on one argument in the Clause Debate (S24).
- **Access.** A door opens: a meeting, a warehouse, a file, a board member's private dining room.
- **Cover.** The corporation's security looks the other way for one scene.
- **Proxy.** In S23, 2 Leverage against an umbrella lets the party demand its director's proxy. The director may refuse, but must then be dealt with in person.

**Losing Leverage.** Leverage is lost when the party is bought off (accepting a “bonus” from the target), when their evidence is destroyed, or when they use it and the target retaliates—at your discretion, one point spent in a heavy-handed way costs one more.

### The Commons Tally

The Grand Ledger records only written agreements. Handshakes, favours done for free, gifts, promises made in a tavern and kept—these are invisible to it. Creation & Company can't see them, can't buy them and can't cancel them.

Whenever the party helps someone and refuses payment, or makes an unwritten promise and keeps it, record that person or group on the **Commons Tally**. Named NPCs count as one; a union, a neighbourhood or a guild counts as one. Most side contracts note where a Tally ally can be won instead of gold.

- **In play.** Tally allies show up. Call on one per session for a small favour—no roll.
- **In S23.** Tally allies attend the Extraordinary General Meeting as observers. For every five, one wavering director has disadvantage to refuse the party.
- **In S24.** Each Tally ally present can act as a **witness**. Clauses with no Receipt can be voided by three witnesses in place of a DC 25 argument. The *Held in Common* ending requires at least twenty.

### Receipts

A Receipt is proof. Twelve spine missions each produce one specific Receipt that voids one clause of the Final Contract; a thirteenth (Incident Report 77-B) is found in the first hour of the campaign and voids the clause that makes the party signatories. Other missions produce Receipts that weaken Tallis Vend or open the way forward.

Receipts are physical. Put them on index cards and hand them to the players. Lose track of one and it's gone; destroyed Receipts can be reconstructed only by a DC 25 argument or three Tally witnesses. By level 20 the players should have a stack of cards they've been carrying for a year, and should suddenly understand why.

### Folios

A Folio is a branch ledger: a lesser book that synchronises with the Grand Ledger. Every major corporation keeps them. Entries written in a Folio become true within that corporation's holdings. The party's first Folio is found in S10 (Wand-Mart); see Appendix B for the magic item. Folios are how the party learns the rules of the Ledger before they ever see it—and how you can dramatise “paperwork is reality” at every level.

### The Board

Creation & Company rules through a Board of ten directors, one per umbrella corporation, plus the Chairman. Tallis Vend controls 51% through their proxies. In S23, the party needs **six seats** flipped (the director votes against Vend) or removed (the director is gone and their proxy void). Each umbrella's dossier in Chapter 5 lists exactly how its seat turns. Track the Board on the Contractor File from S8 onward; the players should be able to see the vote coming for two whole acts.
